But I don’t know if that’s worry or just… me getting stuck on details.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure. I don’t think I walk around feeling panicky or anything like that. It’s more like… I can get stuck on small things, like I said, and then I’m thinking about that instead of whatever I meant to do.\n\nLike yesterday I noticed the hallway rug was crooked, and then somehow I was thinking about whether the floor under it needs cleaning, and then I’d lost track of what I’d gone upstairs for. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety, exactly. Maybe you mean something different by anxious?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t really been tracking it closely. My clothes seem mostly the same, I guess. Sometimes I’ll feel like my jeans are tighter, but then I realize they just came out of the dryer, and that’s its own whole annoying thing because the laundry room is cramped and the vent gets lint everywhere.\n\nBut, um… no, I haven’t noticed a big weight change.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Nervous or on edge… I don’t know. Not in a constant way, I don’t think. I mean, I can feel kind of keyed up if there’s a lot happening around me, or if I’m trying to do something and there’s some little thing distracting me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess the main thing is I’m just worn down almost all the time. Like, even if I sleep a full night, I still wake up feeling like I’m already behind. And then by the time I get through work and try to deal with the house—there’s always clutter, mail, laundry, random things on the counters—I don’t really have much left in me for anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still do them sometimes, but it’s not the same. Most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling running in the background. I can go to the lab and talk to people and do what I’m supposed to do, but things don’t really feel light or exciting anymore.\n\nLike baking—I used to actually enjoy planning what to make, and checking on the telescope used to give me this little spark, you know? Now it’s more like… I do it, but I’m not really feeling much from it. And then I’ll get distracted by some random thing, like needing to clean the counter before I can even start, and then the whole thing just feels heavier than it should.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess, but not like before. Baking still sounds nice in theory, and sometimes I’ll check on the telescope, but most days there’s just this low, gray mood in the background. Like I can still go to the lab and talk to people and look normal, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things anymore.\n\nAnd then I’ll get sidetracked by something stupid, like a dish in the sink or realizing I forgot to put away flour from the last time, and suddenly it’s not relaxing, it’s just… another thing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low, gray mood?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been a little over two years now. It kind of started not long after we moved into this older house, which sounds silly because the house is fine, it just has all these little things that always need attention—like one drawer sticks, and there’s this weird corner where dust collects no matter what I do.\n\nBut yeah, the low, gray feeling… that’s been sort of the baseline since shortly after that move.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, worried, sure, sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. It’s more like I get caught up in little things, and then my mind sort of wanders off.\n\nLike if something in the house is out of place, I’ll notice it, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I should fix that first before doing whatever I was actually going to do. But I don’t know if that’s worry or just… me getting stuck on details.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure. I don’t think I walk around feeling panicky or anything like that. It’s more like… I can get stuck on small things, like I said, and then I’m thinking about that instead of whatever I meant to do.\n\nLike yesterday I noticed the hallway rug was crooked, and then somehow I was thinking about whether the floor under it needs cleaning, and then I’d lost track of what I’d gone upstairs for. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety, exactly. So… I’m not sure I’d say I often feel nervous. Maybe sometimes, but I don’t really know how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel completely uninterested in?\n\nPatient: Completely uninterested… I don’t know if I’d say completely. I still do things. I go to the lab, I talk to people, I can get through the day.\n\nBut most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like, things I used to look forward to—baking, or checking the telescope, even just seeing what came out right or noticing something interesting—don’t feel as light, I guess. I’ll still think, “Oh, I should make bread,” and then I end up staring at the flour container because the lid never sits right, and then it feels like too much trouble.\n\nSo… not totally uninterested, maybe, but the excitement is pretty muted now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened or things you've done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… um, I don’t know. I mean, I’ll replay things sometimes, like if I said something awkward or forgot to respond to an email, but I’m not sure that’s guilt exactly. It’s more like my brain keeps picking at it.\n\nLike last week I spent way too long thinking about whether I’d closed a cabinet in the lab, and then that turned into remembering this whole thing with a drawer that sticks because someone put tape on the runner—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI don’t think I feel guilty a lot, at least not in some big way. I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Completely stopped… yeah, sort of. I mean, I haven’t stopped everything. I’m still getting my lab work done, mostly. But things are slower than they should be—grant drafts, little routine project decisions, those take me much longer now.

At home, though, I’ve kind of stopped keeping up with the things that used to give my evenings shape. Baking, the bonsai, making telescope plans. I’ll think about doing them, and then somehow I don’t. So… yes, some things I used to enjoy have just sort of fallen away.

So… I’m not sure I’d say I often feel nervous. I’ll still think, “Oh, I should make bread,” and then I end up staring at the flour container because the lid never sits right, and then it feels like too much trouble.\n\nSo… not totally uninterested, maybe, but the excitement is pretty muted now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened or things you've done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… um, I don’t know. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing because you no longer find them enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… yeah, sort of. I mean, I haven’t stopped everything. I’m still getting my lab work done, mostly. Yeah… I do, I think. Not in a dramatic way, more like I catch myself assuming things won’t really get back to normal.

Like the house—I keep thinking it’s never going to feel properly settled, which is probably silly because it’s just boxes and little repairs and the one closet door that doesn’t close evenly. But then that turns into, “Well, I’m probably just going to keep functioning at this reduced level,” and then I don’t really plan ahead.

I used to like planning trips or stargazing projects, checking dates and sky conditions and all that, and now I sort of stop before I start because I assume I won’t follow through anyway. So… yes, I guess I do worry about the future, or maybe I expect less from it.

It’s more like my brain keeps picking at it.\n\nLike last week I spent way too long thinking about whether I’d closed a cabinet in the lab, and then that turned into remembering this whole thing with a drawer that sticks because someone put tape on the runner—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI don’t think I feel guilty a lot, at least not in some big way. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing because you no longer find them enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… yeah, sort of. I mean, I haven’t stopped everything. I’m still getting my lab work done, mostly. But things are slower than they should be—grant drafts, little routine project decisions, those take me much longer now.\n\nAt home, though, I’ve kind of stopped keeping up with the things that used to give my evenings shape. Baking, the bonsai, making telescope plans. I’ll think about doing them, and then somehow I don’t. Like the bonsai tools are still in the same place, which bothers me because one of the trays is slightly crooked, but I still don’t actually do anything with them.\n\nSo… yes, some things I used to enjoy have just sort of fallen away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I think. But then that turns into, “Well, I’m probably just going to keep functioning at this reduced level,” and then I don’t really plan ahead.\n\nI used to like planning trips or stargazing projects, checking dates and sky conditions and all that, and now I sort of stop before I start because I assume I won’t follow through anyway. Overwhelmed by fear… I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. Everyday situations can feel like more than they should, but I don’t know that it’s fear exactly.

Like, if there’s a repair person coming by or I have to sort out some house thing, I’ll overthink the timing and whether the hallway is clear enough—actually the hallway still has that stack of boxes I keep meaning to move, which is annoying because it makes the whole place feel unfinished. So… apprehensive sometimes, maybe, but not really overwhelmed by fear.

I still do things. I go to the lab, I talk to people, I can get through the day.\n\nBut most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like, things I used to look forward to—baking, or checking the telescope, even just seeing what came out right or noticing something interesting—don’t feel as light, I guess. I’ll still think, “Oh, I should make bread,” and then I end up staring at the flour container because the lid never sits right, and then it feels like too much trouble.\n\nSo… not totally uninterested, maybe, but the excitement is pretty muted now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened or things you've done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… um, I don’t know. I mean, I’ll replay things sometimes, like if I said something awkward or forgot to respond to an email, but I’m not sure that’s guilt exactly. It’s more like my brain keeps picking at it.\n\nLike last week I spent way too long thinking about whether I’d closed a cabinet in the lab, and then that turned into remembering this whole thing with a drawer that sticks because someone put tape on the runner—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI don’t think I feel guilty a lot, at least not in some big way. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing because you no longer find them enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… yeah, sort of. I mean, I haven’t stopped everything. I’m still getting my lab work done, mostly. But things are slower than they should be—grant drafts, little routine project decisions, those take me much longer now.\n\nAt home, though, I’ve kind of stopped keeping up with the things that used to give my evenings shape. Baking, the bonsai, making telescope plans. I’ll think about doing them, and then somehow I don’t. Like the bonsai tools are still in the same place, which bothers me because one of the trays is slightly crooked, but I still don’t actually do anything with them.\n\nSo… yes, some things I used to enjoy have just sort of fallen away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I think. Not in a dramatic way, more like I catch myself assuming things won’t really get back to normal.\n\nLike the house—I keep thinking it’s never going to feel properly settled, which is probably silly because it’s just boxes and little repairs and the one closet door that doesn’t close evenly. But then that turns into, “Well, I’m probably just going to keep functioning at this reduced level,” and then I don’t really plan ahead.\n\nI used to like planning trips or stargazing projects, checking dates and sky conditions and all that, and now I sort of stop before I start because I assume I won’t follow through anyway. So… yes, I guess I do worry about the future, or maybe I expect less from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely lost pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Completely lost pleasure… I don’t know if I’d say completely. Maybe you mean something different by anxious?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t really been tracking it closely. My clothes seem mostly the same, I guess. Sometimes I’ll feel like my jeans are tighter, but then I realize they just came out of the dryer, and that’s its own whole annoying thing because the laundry room is cramped and the vent gets lint everywhere.\n\nBut, um… no, I haven’t noticed a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Nervous or on edge… I don’t know. Not in a constant way, I don’t think. I mean, I can feel kind of keyed up if there’s a lot happening around me, or if I’m trying to do something and there’s some little thing distracting me.\n\nLike even here, I noticed the chair leg is a little uneven, and then I start thinking about whether it’s the floor or the chair, which is silly, because that’s not what you asked. So… I’m not sure I’d say I often feel nervous. Maybe sometimes, but I don’t really know how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel completely uninterested in?\n\nPatient: Completely uninterested… I don’t know if I’d say completely. I still do things. I go to the lab, I talk to people, I can get through the day.\n\nBut most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like, things I used to look forward to—baking, or checking the telescope, even just seeing what came out right or noticing something interesting—don’t feel as light, I guess. I’ll still think, “Oh, I should make bread,” and then I end up staring at the flour container because the lid never sits right, and then it feels like too much trouble.\n\nSo… not totally uninterested, maybe, but the excitement is pretty muted now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened or things you've done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… um, I don’t know. I mean, I’ll replay things sometimes, like if I said something awkward or forgot to respond to an email, but I’m not sure that’s guilt exactly. It’s more like my brain keeps picking at it.\n\nLike last week I spent way too long thinking about whether I’d closed a cabinet in the lab, and then that turned into remembering this whole thing with a drawer that sticks because someone put tape on the runner—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI don’t think I feel guilty a lot, at least not in some big way. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing because you no longer find them enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… yeah, sort of. I mean, I haven’t stopped everything. I’m still getting my lab work done, mostly. But things are slower than they should be—grant drafts, little routine project decisions, those take me much longer now.\n\nAt home, though, I’ve kind of stopped keeping up with the things that used to give my evenings shape. Baking, the bonsai, making telescope plans. I’ll think about doing them, and then somehow I don’t. Like the bonsai tools are still in the same place, which bothers me because one of the trays is slightly crooked, but I still don’t actually do anything with them.\n\nSo… yes, some things I used to enjoy have just sort of fallen away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I think. Not in a dramatic way, more like I catch myself assuming things won’t really get back to normal.\n\nLike the house—I keep thinking it’s never going to feel properly settled, which is probably silly because it’s just boxes and little repairs and the one closet door that doesn’t close evenly. But then that turns into, “Well, I’m probably just going to keep functioning at this reduced level,” and then I don’t really plan ahead.\n\nI used to like planning trips or stargazing projects, checking dates and sky conditions and all that, and now I sort of stop before I start because I assume I won’t follow through anyway. So… yes, I guess I do worry about the future, or maybe I expect less from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely lost pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Completely lost pleasure… I don’t know if I’d say completely. It’s more like most days there’s this low, gray feeling kind of running in the background.\n\nI can still go to the lab, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do mostly. But things that used to make me feel lighter—like baking, or checking the telescope, even just looking at the sky conditions—don’t really give me that little spark anymore. I’ll think, “Oh, I should do that,” and then it just feels flat.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering exactly. I wouldn’t say everything is gone, but the excitement is definitely… muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by fear or apprehension about everyday situations?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed by fear… I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. Yeah… I think so. Not like I’m crying all the time or anything dramatic, but most days there’s this low, gray mood in the background.

I can still go to the lab and talk to people and get through what I need to do. It’s just… things don’t feel light the way they used to. I can go to the lab and talk to people and do what I’m supposed to do, but things don’t really feel light or exciting anymore.\n\nLike baking—I used to actually enjoy planning what to make, and checking on the telescope used to give me this little spark, you know? Now it’s more like… I do it, but I’m not really feeling much from it. And then I’ll get distracted by some random thing, like needing to clean the counter before I can even start, and then the whole thing just feels heavier than it should.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess, but not like before. Baking still sounds nice in theory, and sometimes I’ll check on the telescope, but most days there’s just this low, gray mood in the background. Like I can still go to the lab and talk to people and look normal, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things anymore.\n\nAnd then I’ll get sidetracked by something stupid, like a dish in the sink or realizing I forgot to put away flour from the last time, and suddenly it’s not relaxing, it’s just… another thing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low, gray mood?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been a little over two years now. It kind of started not long after we moved into this older house, which sounds silly because the house is fine, it just has all these little things that always need attention—like one drawer sticks, and there’s this weird corner where dust collects no matter what I do.\n\nBut yeah, the low, gray feeling… that’s been sort of the baseline since shortly after that move.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, worried, sure, sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. It’s more like I get caught up in little things, and then my mind sort of wanders off.\n\nLike if something in the house is out of place, I’ll notice it, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I should fix that first before doing whatever I was actually going to do. But I don’t know if that’s worry or just… me getting stuck on details.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure. I don’t think I walk around feeling panicky or anything like that. It’s more like… I can get stuck on small things, like I said, and then I’m thinking about that instead of whatever I meant to do.\n\nLike yesterday I noticed the hallway rug was crooked, and then somehow I was thinking about whether the floor under it needs cleaning, and then I’d lost track of what I’d gone upstairs for. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety, exactly. Maybe you mean something different by anxious?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t really been tracking it closely. My clothes seem mostly the same, I guess. Sometimes I’ll feel like my jeans are tighter, but then I realize they just came out of the dryer, and that’s its own whole annoying thing because the laundry room is cramped and the vent gets lint everywhere.\n\nBut, um… no, I haven’t noticed a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Nervous or on edge… I don’t know. Not in a constant way, I don’t think. I mean, I can feel kind of keyed up if there’s a lot happening around me, or if I’m trying to do something and there’s some little thing distracting me.\n\nLike even here, I noticed the chair leg is a little uneven, and then I start thinking about whether it’s the floor or the chair, which is silly, because that’s not what you asked. So… I’m not sure I’d say I often feel nervous. Maybe sometimes, but I don’t really know how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel completely uninterested in?\n\nPatient: Completely uninterested… I don’t know if I’d say completely. I still do things. I go to the lab, I talk to people, I can get through the day.\n\nBut most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like, things I used to look forward to—baking, or checking the telescope, even just seeing what came out right or noticing something interesting—don’t feel as light, I guess. I’ll still think, “Oh, I should make bread,” and then I end up staring at the flour container because the lid never sits right, and then it feels like too much trouble.\n\nSo… not totally uninterested, maybe, but the excitement is pretty muted now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened or things you've done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… um, I don’t know. I mean, I’ll replay things sometimes, like if I said something awkward or forgot to respond to an email, but I’m not sure that’s guilt exactly. It’s more like my brain keeps picking at it.\n\nLike last week I spent way too long thinking about whether I’d closed a cabinet in the lab, and then that turned into remembering this whole thing with a drawer that sticks because someone put tape on the runner—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI don’t think I feel guilty a lot, at least not in some big way. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing because you no longer find them enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… yeah, sort of. I mean, I haven’t stopped everything. I’m still getting my lab work done, mostly. But things are slower than they should be—grant drafts, little routine project decisions, those take me much longer now.\n\nAt home, though, I’ve kind of stopped keeping up with the things that used to give my evenings shape. Baking, the bonsai, making telescope plans. I’ll think about doing them, and then somehow I don’t. Like the bonsai tools are still in the same place, which bothers me because one of the trays is slightly crooked, but I still don’t actually do anything with them.\n\nSo… yes, some things I used to enjoy have just sort of fallen away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I think. Not in a dramatic way, more like I catch myself assuming things won’t really get back to normal.\n\nLike the house—I keep thinking it’s never going to feel properly settled, which is probably silly because it’s just boxes and little repairs and the one closet door that doesn’t close evenly. But then that turns into, “Well, I’m probably just going to keep functioning at this reduced level,” and then I don’t really plan ahead.\n\nI used to like planning trips or stargazing projects, checking dates and sky conditions and all that, and now I sort of stop before I start because I assume I won’t follow through anyway. So… yes, I guess I do worry about the future, or maybe I expect less from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely lost pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Completely lost pleasure… I don’t know if I’d say completely. It’s more like most days there’s this low, gray feeling kind of running in the background.\n\nI can still go to the lab, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do mostly. But things that used to make me feel lighter—like baking, or checking the telescope, even just looking at the sky conditions—don’t really give me that little spark anymore. I’ll think, “Oh, I should do that,” and then it just feels flat.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering exactly. I wouldn’t say everything is gone, but the excitement is definitely… muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by fear or apprehension about everyday situations?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed by fear… I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. Everyday situations can feel like more than they should, but I don’t know that it’s fear exactly.\n\nLike, if there’s a repair person coming by or I have to sort out some house thing, I’ll overthink the timing and whether the hallway is clear enough—actually the hallway still has that stack of boxes I keep meaning to move, which is annoying because it makes the whole place feel unfinished. But I don’t usually feel scared, exactly.\n\nSo… apprehensive sometimes, maybe, but not really overwhelmed by fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it closely, though. The scale is in the bathroom, but it’s sort of wedged near this box of cleaning stuff I still haven’t unpacked, so I don’t step on it much. But no, I don’t think I’ve lost a significant amount over the past couple years.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like I’m crying all the time or anything dramatic, but most days there’s this low, gray mood in the background.\n\nI can still go to the lab and talk to people and get through what I need to do. It’s just… things don’t feel light the way they used to. Like baking, or checking on the telescope—actually I used to really look forward to seeing how the sky conditions were, even if it was just routine—and now it’s more like I do it, or think about doing it, and there’s not much feeling there.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do feel sad most of the time, in a kind of muted way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Like baking, or checking on the telescope—actually I used to really look forward to seeing how the sky conditions were, even if it was just routine—and now it’s more like I do it, or think about doing it, and there’s not much feeling there.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do feel sad most of the time, in a kind of muted way.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you still enjoy and find pleasure in?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 39 / physical_scientist / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days there’s this low, gray mood running in the background, like I can still go to the lab and talk to people, but I don’t really feel light or excited about things like baking or checking the telescope the way I used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Some things, maybe a little. I still do them, at least. Baking, sometimes, or checking on the telescope stuff, but it’s not the same kind of pleasure I used to get from it.

Most days there’s just this low, gray mood running in the background. I can go to the lab and talk to people and get through the day, but I don’t really feel light or excited about things. Even with baking—like I’ll think about making something, and then I remember the mixing bowls are in that awkward cabinet, and I should really reorganize it—and by then it just feels like effort instead of fun.

So… I guess I still sort of enjoy a few things, but it’s muted. Not like before.

Baking still sounds nice in theory, and sometimes I’ll check on the telescope, but most days there’s just this low, gray mood in the background. Like I can still go to the lab and talk to people and look normal, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things anymore.\n\nAnd then I’ll get sidetracked by something stupid, like a dish in the sink or realizing I forgot to put away flour from the last time, and suddenly it’s not relaxing, it’s just… another thing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low, gray mood?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been a little over two years now. It’s more like I get caught up in little things, and then my mind sort of wanders off.\n\nLike if something in the house is out of place, I’ll notice it, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I should fix that first before doing whatever I was actually going to do. But I don’t know if that’s worry or just… me getting stuck on details.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure. I don’t think I walk around feeling panicky or anything like that. It’s more like… I can get stuck on small things, like I said, and then I’m thinking about that instead of whatever I meant to do.\n\nLike yesterday I noticed the hallway rug was crooked, and then somehow I was thinking about whether the floor under it needs cleaning, and then I’d lost track of what I’d gone upstairs for. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety, exactly. Maybe you mean something different by anxious?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t really been tracking it closely. My clothes seem mostly the same, I guess. Sometimes I’ll feel like my jeans are tighter, but then I realize they just came out of the dryer, and that’s its own whole annoying thing because the laundry room is cramped and the vent gets lint everywhere.\n\nBut, um… no, I haven’t noticed a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Nervous or on edge… I don’t know. So… I’m not sure I’d say I often feel nervous. I still do things. I go to the lab, I talk to people, I can get through the day.\n\nBut most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like, things I used to look forward to—baking, or checking the telescope, even just seeing what came out right or noticing something interesting—don’t feel as light, I guess. I’ll still think, “Oh, I should make bread,” and then I end up staring at the flour container because the lid never sits right, and then it feels like too much trouble.\n\nSo… not totally uninterested, maybe, but the excitement is pretty muted now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened or things you've done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… um, I don’t know. I mean, I’ll replay things sometimes, like if I said something awkward or forgot to respond to an email, but I’m not sure that’s guilt exactly. It’s more like my brain keeps picking at it.\n\nLike last week I spent way too long thinking about whether I’d closed a cabinet in the lab, and then that turned into remembering this whole thing with a drawer that sticks because someone put tape on the runner—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI don’t think I feel guilty a lot, at least not in some big way. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing because you no longer find them enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… yeah, sort of. I mean, I haven’t stopped everything. I’m still getting my lab work done, mostly. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess the main thing is I’m just worn down almost all the time. Like, even if I sleep a full night, I still wake up feeling like I’m already behind. And then by the time I get through work and try to deal with the house—there’s always clutter, mail, laundry, random things on the counters—I don’t really have much left in me for anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still do them sometimes, but it’s not the same. Most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling running in the background. I can go to the lab and talk to people and do what I’m supposed to do, but things don’t really feel light or exciting anymore.\n\nLike baking—I used to actually enjoy planning what to make, and checking on the telescope used to give me this little spark, you know? Now it’s more like… I do it, but I’m not really feeling much from it. And then I’ll get distracted by some random thing, like needing to clean the counter before I can even start, and then the whole thing just feels heavier than it should.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess, but not like before. Baking still sounds nice in theory, and sometimes I’ll check on the telescope, but most days there’s just this low, gray mood in the background. Like I can still go to the lab and talk to people and look normal, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things anymore.\n\nAnd then I’ll get sidetracked by something stupid, like a dish in the sink or realizing I forgot to put away flour from the last time, and suddenly it’s not relaxing, it’s just… another thing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low, gray mood?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been a little over two years now. It kind of started not long after we moved into this older house, which sounds silly because the house is fine, it just has all these little things that always need attention—like one drawer sticks, and there’s this weird corner where dust collects no matter what I do.\n\nBut yeah, the low, gray feeling… that’s been sort of the baseline since shortly after that move.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, worried, sure, sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. It’s more like I get caught up in little things, and then my mind sort of wanders off.\n\nLike if something in the house is out of place, I’ll notice it, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I should fix that first before doing whatever I was actually going to do. But I don’t know if that’s worry or just… me getting stuck on details.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure. I don’t think I walk around feeling panicky or anything like that. It’s more like… I can get stuck on small things, like I said, and then I’m thinking about that instead of whatever I meant to do.\n\nLike yesterday I noticed the hallway rug was crooked, and then somehow I was thinking about whether the floor under it needs cleaning, and then I’d lost track of what I’d gone upstairs for. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety, exactly. Maybe you mean something different by anxious?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t really been tracking it closely. My clothes seem mostly the same, I guess. Sometimes I’ll feel like my jeans are tighter, but then I realize they just came out of the dryer, and that’s its own whole annoying thing because the laundry room is cramped and the vent gets lint everywhere.\n\nBut, um… no, I haven’t noticed a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Nervous or on edge… I don’t know. Not in a constant way, I don’t think. I mean, I can feel kind of keyed up if there’s a lot happening around me, or if I’m trying to do something and there’s some little thing distracting me.\n\nLike even here, I noticed the chair leg is a little uneven, and then I start thinking about whether it’s the floor or the chair, which is silly, because that’s not what you asked. So… I’m not sure I’d say I often feel nervous. Maybe sometimes, but I don’t really know how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel completely uninterested in?\n\nPatient: Completely uninterested… I don’t know if I’d say completely. I still do things. I go to the lab, I talk to people, I can get through the day.\n\nBut most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like, things I used to look forward to—baking, or checking the telescope, even just seeing what came out right or noticing something interesting—don’t feel as light, I guess. I’ll still think, “Oh, I should make bread,” and then I end up staring at the flour container because the lid never sits right, and then it feels like too much trouble.\n\nSo… not totally uninterested, maybe, but the excitement is pretty muted now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened or things you've done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… um, I don’t know. I mean, I’ll replay things sometimes, like if I said something awkward or forgot to respond to an email, but I’m not sure that’s guilt exactly. It’s more like my brain keeps picking at it.\n\nLike last week I spent way too long thinking about whether I’d closed a cabinet in the lab, and then that turned into remembering this whole thing with a drawer that sticks because someone put tape on the runner—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI don’t think I feel guilty a lot, at least not in some big way. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing because you no longer find them enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… yeah, sort of. I mean, I haven’t stopped everything. I’m still getting my lab work done, mostly. But things are slower than they should be—grant drafts, little routine project decisions, those take me much longer now.\n\nAt home, though, I’ve kind of stopped keeping up with the things that used to give my evenings shape. Baking, the bonsai, making telescope plans. I’ll think about doing them, and then somehow I don’t. Like the bonsai tools are still in the same place, which bothers me because one of the trays is slightly crooked, but I still don’t actually do anything with them.\n\nSo… yes, some things I used to enjoy have just sort of fallen away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I think. Not in a dramatic way, more like I catch myself assuming things won’t really get back to normal.\n\nLike the house—I keep thinking it’s never going to feel properly settled, which is probably silly because it’s just boxes and little repairs and the one closet door that doesn’t close evenly. But then that turns into, “Well, I’m probably just going to keep functioning at this reduced level,” and then I don’t really plan ahead.\n\nI used to like planning trips or stargazing projects, checking dates and sky conditions and all that, and now I sort of stop before I start because I assume I won’t follow through anyway. So… yes, I guess I do worry about the future, or maybe I expect less from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely lost pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Completely lost pleasure… I don’t know if I’d say completely. It’s more like most days there’s this low, gray feeling kind of running in the background.\n\nI can still go to the lab, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do mostly. But things that used to make me feel lighter—like baking, or checking the telescope, even just looking at the sky conditions—don’t really give me that little spark anymore. I’ll think, “Oh, I should do that,” and then it just feels flat.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering exactly. I wouldn’t say everything is gone, but the excitement is definitely… muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by fear or apprehension about everyday situations?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed by fear… I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. Everyday situations can feel like more than they should, but I don’t know that it’s fear exactly.\n\nLike, if there’s a repair person coming by or I have to sort out some house thing, I’ll overthink the timing and whether the hallway is clear enough—actually the hallway still has that stack of boxes I keep meaning to move, which is annoying because it makes the whole place feel unfinished. But I don’t usually feel scared, exactly.\n\nSo… apprehensive sometimes, maybe, but not really overwhelmed by fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Moderate, I guess. Not severe. I’m still getting my lab work done, so from the outside it probably looks mostly fine.

But things take longer than they should. Grant drafts especially—I’ll sit with them open and sort of circle around the same paragraph, or routine project decisions that should be quick feel like… I don’t know, like wading through something.

And at home it shows up more. I used to have these little plans that gave shape to my downtime—baking something on the weekend, working on the bonsai, checking telescope plans or sky conditions—and lately I just don’t keep up with them. I think about it, and then it sort of slips away. So… moderate, probably. Not falling apart, but definitely affected.

I can go to the lab and talk to people and do what I’m supposed to do, but things don’t really feel light or exciting anymore.\n\nLike baking—I used to actually enjoy planning what to make, and checking on the telescope used to give me this little spark, you know? Now it’s more like… I do it, but I’m not really feeling much from it. And then I’ll get distracted by some random thing, like needing to clean the counter before I can even start, and then the whole thing just feels heavier than it should.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess, but not like before. Baking still sounds nice in theory, and sometimes I’ll check on the telescope, but most days there’s just this low, gray mood in the background. Like I can still go to the lab and talk to people and look normal, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things anymore.\n\nAnd then I’ll get sidetracked by something stupid, like a dish in the sink or realizing I forgot to put away flour from the last time, and suddenly it’s not relaxing, it’s just… another thing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low, gray mood?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been a little over two years now. It kind of started not long after we moved into this older house, which sounds silly because the house is fine, it just has all these little things that always need attention—like one drawer sticks, and there’s this weird corner where dust collects no matter what I do.\n\nBut yeah, the low, gray feeling… that’s been sort of the baseline since shortly after that move.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, worried, sure, sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. It’s more like I get caught up in little things, and then my mind sort of wanders off.\n\nLike if something in the house is out of place, I’ll notice it, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I should fix that first before doing whatever I was actually going to do. But I don’t know if that’s worry or just… me getting stuck on details.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure. I don’t think I walk around feeling panicky or anything like that. It’s more like… I can get stuck on small things, like I said, and then I’m thinking about that instead of whatever I meant to do.\n\nLike yesterday I noticed the hallway rug was crooked, and then somehow I was thinking about whether the floor under it needs cleaning, and then I’d lost track of what I’d gone upstairs for. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety, exactly. Maybe you mean something different by anxious?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t really been tracking it closely. My clothes seem mostly the same, I guess. Sometimes I’ll feel like my jeans are tighter, but then I realize they just came out of the dryer, and that’s its own whole annoying thing because the laundry room is cramped and the vent gets lint everywhere.\n\nBut, um… no, I haven’t noticed a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Nervous or on edge… I don’t know. Not in a constant way, I don’t think. I mean, I can feel kind of keyed up if there’s a lot happening around me, or if I’m trying to do something and there’s some little thing distracting me.\n\nLike even here, I noticed the chair leg is a little uneven, and then I start thinking about whether it’s the floor or the chair, which is silly, because that’s not what you asked. So… I’m not sure I’d say I often feel nervous. Maybe sometimes, but I don’t really know how to measure that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel completely uninterested in?\n\nPatient: Completely uninterested… I don’t know if I’d say completely. I still do things. I go to the lab, I talk to people, I can get through the day.\n\nBut most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background. Like, things I used to look forward to—baking, or checking the telescope, even just seeing what came out right or noticing something interesting—don’t feel as light, I guess. I’ll still think, “Oh, I should make bread,” and then I end up staring at the flour container because the lid never sits right, and then it feels like too much trouble.\n\nSo… not totally uninterested, maybe, but the excitement is pretty muted now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened or things you've done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… um, I don’t know. I mean, I’ll replay things sometimes, like if I said something awkward or forgot to respond to an email, but I’m not sure that’s guilt exactly. It’s more like my brain keeps picking at it.\n\nLike last week I spent way too long thinking about whether I’d closed a cabinet in the lab, and then that turned into remembering this whole thing with a drawer that sticks because someone put tape on the runner—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI don’t think I feel guilty a lot, at least not in some big way. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing because you no longer find them enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… yeah, sort of. I mean, I haven’t stopped everything. I’m still getting my lab work done, mostly. But things are slower than they should be—grant drafts, little routine project decisions, those take me much longer now.\n\nAt home, though, I’ve kind of stopped keeping up with the things that used to give my evenings shape. Baking, the bonsai, making telescope plans. I’ll think about doing them, and then somehow I don’t. Like the bonsai tools are still in the same place, which bothers me because one of the trays is slightly crooked, but I still don’t actually do anything with them.\n\nSo… yes, some things I used to enjoy have just sort of fallen away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I think. Not in a dramatic way, more like I catch myself assuming things won’t really get back to normal.\n\nLike the house—I keep thinking it’s never going to feel properly settled, which is probably silly because it’s just boxes and little repairs and the one closet door that doesn’t close evenly. But then that turns into, “Well, I’m probably just going to keep functioning at this reduced level,” and then I don’t really plan ahead.\n\nI used to like planning trips or stargazing projects, checking dates and sky conditions and all that, and now I sort of stop before I start because I assume I won’t follow through anyway. So… yes, I guess I do worry about the future, or maybe I expect less from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely lost pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Completely lost pleasure… I don’t know if I’d say completely. It’s more like most days there’s this low, gray feeling kind of running in the background.\n\nI can still go to the lab, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do mostly. But things that used to make me feel lighter—like baking, or checking the telescope, even just looking at the sky conditions—don’t really give me that little spark anymore. I’ll think, “Oh, I should do that,” and then it just feels flat.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering exactly. I wouldn’t say everything is gone, but the excitement is definitely… muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by fear or apprehension about everyday situations?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed by fear… I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. Everyday situations can feel like more than they should, but I don’t know that it’s fear exactly.\n\nLike, if there’s a repair person coming by or I have to sort out some house thing, I’ll overthink the timing and whether the hallway is clear enough—actually the hallway still has that stack of boxes I keep meaning to move, which is annoying because it makes the whole place feel unfinished. But I don’t usually feel scared, exactly.\n\nSo… apprehensive sometimes, maybe, but not really overwhelmed by fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it closely, though. The scale is in the bathroom, but it’s sort of wedged near this box of cleaning stuff I still haven’t unpacked, so I don’t step on it much. But no, I don’t think I’ve lost a significant amount over the past couple years.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like I’m crying all the time or anything dramatic, but most days there’s this low, gray mood in the background.\n\nI can still go to the lab and talk to people and get through what I need to do. It’s just… things don’t feel light the way they used to. Like baking, or checking on the telescope—actually I used to really look forward to seeing how the sky conditions were, even if it was just routine—and now it’s more like I do it, or think about doing it, and there’s not much feeling there.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do feel sad most of the time, in a kind of muted way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy and find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Some things, maybe a little. I still do them, at least. Baking, sometimes, or checking on the telescope stuff, but it’s not the same kind of pleasure I used to get from it.\n\nMost days there’s just this low, gray mood running in the background. I can go to the lab and talk to people and get through the day, but I don’t really feel light or excited about things. Even with baking—like I’ll think about making something, and then I remember the mixing bowls are in that awkward cabinet, and I should really reorganize it—and by then it just feels like effort instead of fun.\n\nSo… I guess I still sort of enjoy a few things, but it’s muted. Not like before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your low, gray mood is mild or more severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’d say mild, most of the time. It’s not like I can’t function—I still go to the lab, talk to people, get through what I need to do.\n\nIt’s more like this low grayness running in the background. Like, I’ll think about baking or checking the telescope, and I know those things used to make me feel kind of interested or happy, but now it’s just… flatter. Not severe exactly, just always sort of there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low, gray mood is affecting your daily life in a moderate or severe way?\n\nPatient: Moderate, I guess. Not severe. I’m still getting my lab work done, so from the outside it probably looks mostly fine.\n\nBut things take longer than they should. Grant drafts especially—I’ll sit with them open and sort of circle around the same paragraph, or routine project decisions that should be quick feel like… I don’t know, like wading through something.\n\nAnd at home it shows up more. I used to have these little plans that gave shape to my downtime—baking something on the weekend, working on the bonsai, checking telescope plans or sky conditions—and lately I just don’t keep up with them. I think about it, and then it sort of slips away. So… moderate, probably. Not falling apart, but definitely affected.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think it’s more the daily activities than relationships, if I’m choosing between those. I’m still getting my lab work done, but it’s slower—grant drafts especially, and even routine project decisions take me longer than they should.

I mean, I’ll replay things sometimes, like if I said something awkward or forgot to respond to an email, but I’m not sure that’s guilt exactly. It’s more like my brain keeps picking at it.\n\nLike last week I spent way too long thinking about whether I’d closed a cabinet in the lab, and then that turned into remembering this whole thing with a drawer that sticks because someone put tape on the runner—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI don’t think I feel guilty a lot, at least not in some big way. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have completely stopped doing because you no longer find them enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… yeah, sort of. I mean, I haven’t stopped everything. I’m still getting my lab work done, mostly. But things are slower than they should be—grant drafts, little routine project decisions, those take me much longer now.\n\nAt home, though, I’ve kind of stopped keeping up with the things that used to give my evenings shape. Baking, the bonsai, making telescope plans. I’ll think about doing them, and then somehow I don’t. Like the bonsai tools are still in the same place, which bothers me because one of the trays is slightly crooked, but I still don’t actually do anything with them.\n\nSo… yes, some things I used to enjoy have just sort of fallen away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, I think. Not in a dramatic way, more like I catch myself assuming things won’t really get back to normal.\n\nLike the house—I keep thinking it’s never going to feel properly settled, which is probably silly because it’s just boxes and little repairs and the one closet door that doesn’t close evenly. But then that turns into, “Well, I’m probably just going to keep functioning at this reduced level,” and then I don’t really plan ahead.\n\nI used to like planning trips or stargazing projects, checking dates and sky conditions and all that, and now I sort of stop before I start because I assume I won’t follow through anyway. So… yes, I guess I do worry about the future, or maybe I expect less from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely lost pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Completely lost pleasure… I don’t know if I’d say completely. It’s more like most days there’s this low, gray feeling kind of running in the background.\n\nI can still go to the lab, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do mostly. But things that used to make me feel lighter—like baking, or checking the telescope, even just looking at the sky conditions—don’t really give me that little spark anymore. I’ll think, “Oh, I should do that,” and then it just feels flat.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering exactly. I wouldn’t say everything is gone, but the excitement is definitely… muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by fear or apprehension about everyday situations?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed by fear… I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. Everyday situations can feel like more than they should, but I don’t know that it’s fear exactly.\n\nLike, if there’s a repair person coming by or I have to sort out some house thing, I’ll overthink the timing and whether the hallway is clear enough—actually the hallway still has that stack of boxes I keep meaning to move, which is annoying because it makes the whole place feel unfinished. But I don’t usually feel scared, exactly.\n\nSo… apprehensive sometimes, maybe, but not really overwhelmed by fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it closely, though. The scale is in the bathroom, but it’s sort of wedged near this box of cleaning stuff I still haven’t unpacked, so I don’t step on it much. But no, I don’t think I’ve lost a significant amount over the past couple years.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like I’m crying all the time or anything dramatic, but most days there’s this low, gray mood in the background.\n\nI can still go to the lab and talk to people and get through what I need to do. It’s just… things don’t feel light the way they used to. Like baking, or checking on the telescope—actually I used to really look forward to seeing how the sky conditions were, even if it was just routine—and now it’s more like I do it, or think about doing it, and there’s not much feeling there.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do feel sad most of the time, in a kind of muted way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy and find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Some things, maybe a little. I still do them, at least. Baking, sometimes, or checking on the telescope stuff, but it’s not the same kind of pleasure I used to get from it.\n\nMost days there’s just this low, gray mood running in the background. I can go to the lab and talk to people and get through the day, but I don’t really feel light or excited about things. Even with baking—like I’ll think about making something, and then I remember the mixing bowls are in that awkward cabinet, and I should really reorganize it—and by then it just feels like effort instead of fun.\n\nSo… I guess I still sort of enjoy a few things, but it’s muted. Not like before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your low, gray mood is mild or more severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’d say mild, most of the time. It’s not like I can’t function—I still go to the lab, talk to people, get through what I need to do.\n\nIt’s more like this low grayness running in the background. Like, I’ll think about baking or checking the telescope, and I know those things used to make me feel kind of interested or happy, but now it’s just… flatter. Not severe exactly, just always sort of there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low, gray mood is affecting your daily life in a moderate or severe way?\n\nPatient: Moderate, I guess. Not severe. I’m still getting my lab work done, so from the outside it probably looks mostly fine.\n\nBut things take longer than they should. Grant drafts especially—I’ll sit with them open and sort of circle around the same paragraph, or routine project decisions that should be quick feel like… I don’t know, like wading through something.\n\nAnd at home it shows up more. I used to have these little plans that gave shape to my downtime—baking something on the weekend, working on the bonsai, checking telescope plans or sky conditions—and lately I just don’t keep up with them. I think about it, and then it sort of slips away. So… moderate, probably. Not falling apart, but definitely affected.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low, gray mood is interfering with your relationships or daily activities more than it is with your interest in activities?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the daily activities than relationships, if I’m choosing between those. I’m still getting my lab work done, but it’s slower—grant drafts especially, and even routine project decisions take me longer than they should.\n\nAnd then at home, it kind of shows up in the things that used to structure my downtime. Like baking, the bonsai, telescope plans… I don’t keep up with them the way I used to. Although then I start thinking, maybe that is “interest,” because those are activities I used to enjoy. But it feels less like I don’t care at all and more like everything takes this extra push, and I just don’t follow through.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
